  builds TEX0 as the kept bits, the size codes, the format (bits 17-14 of
  its table word), then **`0xB << 9` for a texture without levels**, or its
  level value `<< 9` with levels (DoomGLES's `0x...9600` has the `0xB`).
  Version 24 left them 0: no level drew. TEX1 bits 28-25 are a bias
  computed in floats ((bias clamped to -0.4375..2, + 1.75) x 4), not a
  level selector. Where the levels sit in memory is decided in a copy
  helper (`0x02266c60`), not read.

**`rp-mbx3d` version 25 on the device (2026-10-15,
`local/rp-mbx3d-run25.txt`).**

- **T12b, mipmaps: work.** With TEX0 bits 12-9 non-zero (3, 6, 7 or
  `0xB`: all alike) and TEX1 nearest-mipmap (bit 29), **layout B** (the
  base first, each level after the larger one) drew the 16 x 16 level on a
  16 px quad and the 8 x 8 on an 8 px quad (a 64 x 64 texture); layout A
  (the PowerVR2's, smallest first) always drew the base. Version 24 left
  bits 12-9 at 0 and drew only the base: **bits 12-9 are the highest level
  index allowed**.
- **T13b, PVRTC's block order: twiddled like texels** (x in the odd bits):
  each block's centre showed row 0 = blocks 0, 2, 8, 10, row 1 = 1, 3, 9,
  11. Both models had assumed it; now measured.
- **T11b, depth stored across renders: still nothing** with the GLES
  driver's full register set (`0x648`, `0x6D0`, the rest), `n` 16 or
  2400, load bit on or off: the buffer untouched, B drew everywhere. The
  GLES scene routine (`0x0225e5c4`), with a depth buffer, also sets **bit
  19 of the tile accelerator's configuration word** (`0x85C`, ours never
  written on the direct path) and `0x610`'s store bit: depth storage may
  belong to the TA path. **Parked** until the TA path is built for games
  (ADR-0026 D7); then one try with `0x85C` bit 19 settles it. The
  interface doesn't need it (each render's depth starts from the
  background).

## Alpha test, fog, the second texture unit (static analysis, 2026-10-18)

Read from Intel's `libGLES_CL.dll` (ROM A08) because three owned games
use these features and DoomGLES didn't: Call of Duty 2 (`glAlphaFuncx`,
`glFogx`, two texture units, a `GL_LUMINANCE` texture), THPS2 (fog),
Virtual Pool GLES (two units) ([research/gles-games.md](../research/gles-games.md)).
The state words are built at `0x02267700` from the context: TSP at
`+0xF4`, the second word (called "ISP" so far) at `+0xF8`, the enable
flags at `+0x144` (`glEnable`: bit 0 `GL_BLEND`, bit 1 `GL_ALPHA_TEST`,
bit 5 `GL_DEPTH_TEST` (only with a depth buffer), bit 7 `GL_FOG`, bits 8/9
line/point smooth).

| Finding | Confidence | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **The second state word's top 11 bits are the alpha test**: bits 31-29 the comparison in GL order (`GL_NEVER` 0 ... `GL_ALWAYS` 7), bits 28-21 the reference (0-255). `glAlphaFuncx` stores `func << 29 \| ref << 21` at `+0x5E8`; the builder ORs it over the word's low 21 bits. Dell's objects' `0xE0000000` = "always, reference 0"; DoomGLES's `0xEC800000` = "always, reference 100" | **confirmed** (device, `rp-glesfeat` A) | `glAlphaFuncx` `0x02262060`; builder `0x0226770C` |
| **Alpha testing on = TSP bit 14** (`0x4000`), alongside bit 13 for blending (measured, version 23): when `GL_ALPHA_TEST` is on and the comparison can fail, the builder sets bit 14 and clears TSP bit 8; when it is off or always passes, it forces the comparison to `ALWAYS` and sets bits 8-7. Bits 13 and 14 look like PowerVR's object kinds (translucent, punch-through); opaque has neither | likely (code) | `0x022677E0`-`0x02267840` |
| **Fog = TSP bits 25-24**: bit 25 alone (every captured word: Dell's `0x22220E80`, DoomGLES's `0x22640200`) = no fog; `GL_FOG` on sets **bit 24 instead**; the logic-op path sets neither | likely (code) | `0x022677B8` |
| **Fog is worked out on the CPU, per vertex**: `glFog`'s start, end, density (`+0x58C`-`+0x594`) and on/off go to the transform stage (setter `0x0227246C` indexes 3, 6-8), which adds a word to each vertex ("+4 with a second colour or fog", ADR-0024); the chip blends toward the fog colour in **`0x63C`** (from `+0x588`, known) | likely (code) | `0x02267F58`-`0x02267FA0`; `glFogx` `0x0226DE28` |
| **The second texture unit's three words** (TEX0-TEX2 as for unit 0) live at `+0x108`-`+0x110` and follow unit 0's (`+0xFC`-`+0x104`) in the state block when the block's mask bit 3 is set (bit 2 = unit 0); each vertex gets 8 more bytes (u, v) per unit | likely (code) | state-block emitter `0x02267868` |
| **`GL_LUMINANCE` / `GL_UNSIGNED_BYTE` uploads as format code 10** (`0x14000`, 8 bits a texel), `GL_LUMINANCE_ALPHA` as code 8 (`0x10000`, 16 bits); `GL_ALPHA` takes its own path (not read) | confirmed (table; code 10 = 8-bit grey on the device, `rp-glesfeat` L) | `glTexSubImage2D`'s mapping into the table at `0x0225D30C` (entries of 12 bytes, read from their second word) |

**`rp-glesfeat` on the device (2026-10-18, under the loader, WM's clock,
no board bits; every render finished).**

- **A, alpha test: confirmed.** A red square with vertex alpha 64 over
  grey: drawn with "always", "greater 32", "less 128"; hidden with
  "greater 128" and "less 0". **TSP bit 14 changed nothing** (the test
  applies without it; what bit 14 does is open).
- **D, depth "less" works** with the second word's alpha bits "always" and
  the mode only in TSP bits 11-9: blue at z 0.3 drew over red at 0.5 with
  "less" and "less or equal" and stayed behind the background (z 0.1);
  blue at 0.8 lost to red; "greater" the reverse, exactly (smaller z
  passes "less": the usual sense). Version 23's form (`0x20000000`, the
  mode in the alpha bits) hid blue everywhere again.
- **L, format code 10 = 8-bit grey:** texels `0x60` drew `0x606060`,
  `0xC0` drew `0xC0C0C0`; with TEX2 `0x86084610` the pixel's alpha was the
  texel's (`0x60606060`), with `0x86086610` 255: TEX2 bit 13 picks the
  alpha's source (clear: the texture; set: the vertex), as version 23's
  T5c suggested.

**`rp-glesfeat` test M on the device (2026-10-22, the gate's P5,
`local/rp-glesfeat-M.txt`): the second texture unit through the TA.**
Two flat 8 x 8 grey textures (code 10), 0x80 in unit 0 and 0x40 in
unit 1, the state block with mask bit 3 (unit 1's three words after unit
0's: the emitter `0x02267868` confirms that order: mask, TSP, ISP, unit 0,
unit 1, the bit-6 word) and 9-word vertices (u, v for each unit).

- **The one-unit control through the TA draws** (0x80808080) and the TA
  writes the 26-word textured object of our direct form.
- **Mask bit 3 alone: the TA ignores unit 1** (it reads 7-word vertices;
  unit 1's words land as the clip and bit-6 words; the primitive word
  then comes out garbled, type 6). **TSP bit 16 tells the TA there is a
  second coordinate set:** with it the TA writes a **two-unit object**:
  ISP, unit 0's TEX0-2, unit 1's TEX0-2, TSP, x/y x3, z x3, 1/w x3, then
  per vertex colour, u0, v0, u1, v1 (32 words), its list word with
  **layout 12 in bits 24-21** (one unit: 8, flat: 4). TSP bits 19-17 at
  1, 2 or 3 change nothing seen here (Dell's textured draws carry 1,
  DoomGLES's 2; still unknown).
- **The 3D stage then hangs on every two-unit object** (render done never
  set in 200 ms) with unit 1's TEX2 `0x86084E10` (the GLES table's unit-1
  MODULATE), `0x86084610` and `0xD6087E10`. What the 3D stage needs for a
  second unit is open (a register in the 3D table, likely; static
  reading next).
- **After such a hang, a core reset and the memory manager's set-up
  don't bring the TA path back:** the one-unit control hung after each
  two-unit hang in the same run, and drew again at the next image's
  start-up (MBXCLK, reset, set-up). The renderer's own recovery (the
  same reset and set-up) has recovered hung direct-path renders on the
  device (G4c) and doesn't use the TA (quality audit C53).

**The combine word (TEX2) is a table lookup** (static, 2026-10-18;
builder `0x02267434`, tables at `0x0225D3A0` and `+200`). `glTexEnv`'s
`GL_TEXTURE_ENV_MODE` is stored per unit as an index: **MODULATE 0, DECAL
1, BLEND 2, REPLACE 3, ADD 4**, Intel's own modes `0x8C04`-`0x8C09` 5-10,
`GL_DOT3_RGBA` (`0x86AF`) 11 (`glTexEnvxv` `0x02263220`; `GL_TEXTURE_ENV_COLOR`
packed separately). TEX2 = table[unit][kind][mode], where kind (0-4) is
the texture's component class (from its format descriptor). DoomGLES's
captured `0x86086610` is unit 0, MODULATE, kind 1 or 3: the table matches
the game. Unit 0 (unit 1 differs in bits 11 and 7: `...4610` →
`...4E10`, `...6610` → `...6E10`, combine modes also bit 22):

| kind | MODULATE | DECAL | BLEND | REPLACE | ADD | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 | 10 | DOT3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | c6084610 | c6087610 | c6084610 | c6087610 | c6084610 | c6087610 | c6087624 | c6287610 | c6287e10 | c6286610 | c6084610 | c6046608 |
| 1 | 86086610 | d6086610 | 84e06610 | d6086610 | c6806610 | 86086610 | c6086614 | d6c86608 | bfc87e10 | a6886610 | c5046610 | c6046608 |
| 2 | 86084610 | d6086610 | 84e04610 | d6087610 | c6804610 | 86087610 | c6087624 | b6c87610 | bfc87e10 | a6886610 | c5044610 | c6046608 |
| 3 | 86086610 | d6086610 | 84e06610 | d6086610 | c6806610 | 86086610 | c6086614 | d6c86608 | bfc87e10 | a6886610 | c5046610 | 86096612 |
| 4 | 86084610 | b1846610 | 84e04610 | d6087610 | c6804610 | 86087610 | c6087624 | b6c87610 | be887e10 | a6886610 | c5044610 | 86094612 |

So our renderer needs no combine encoder of its own to run legacy games:
the GLES layer can carry this table (facts, as numbers), and decoding its
bit fields is only needed for combines GLES never asks for. Two-unit
lightmaps (Call of Duty 2) = unit 0 REPLACE or MODULATE, unit 1 MODULATE.
**Device tests to confirm** (one
`rp-mbx3d` version, no board bits): a texture with alpha 0/255 halves drawn
with TSP bit 14 and the comparison "greater, 128" (half drawn), then
"always" (all drawn); fog bit 24 with a vertex fog word ramp and `0x63C`
red; a second unit's words with mask bit 3 (two checkers multiplied); an
8-bit grey texture at code 10.

## Texture shapes: rectangles, sizes, row order (static, 2026-10-19)

From `libGLES_CL.dll` (A), read for the 3D compositor proposal
(docs/design/ui.md: a 480 x 640 layer as one texture). Findings only.

| Finding | Confidence | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **Rectangular power-of-two textures:** the driver builds TEX0's two size codes apart, width in bits 27-24, height in 23-20 (`((log2 w - 3) << 4 \| (log2 h - 3)) << 20`); nothing forces them equal. A 480 x 640 layer fits a 512 x 1024 texture (1 MiB at 16 bits) instead of 1024 x 1024 | likely (code read; `rp-gpubench` G4a draws it) | `0x02263924`-`0x02263930`, TEX0 assembled `0x0226576c`-`0x02265794` |
| **A rectangle's texel order:** twiddled (x in the odd bits) over the shorter side's bits, the longer side's remaining bits above them in order: squares of the short side, one after the other along the long side (`tex::twiddle_rect`) | likely (code read) | address function `0x02265a98`-`0x02265b10`, its callers per texel size `0x02265b14`, `0x02265bb0`, `0x02265c64` |
| Sides under 8 are padded to 8 (texels repeated, the LOD bias adjusted) | likely | `0x022638a0`-`0x02263920`, `0x02266a5c`-`0x02266b84` |
| **No row-order (non-twiddled, strided) texture mode in the driver:** TEX0 is rebuilt through the mask `0xF00C21FF` and no path sets bits 19-18, 13 or 8-0; no stride is written for textures; the format table holds only the 12 known rows; every upload goes linear system copy, then the twiddle writer | likely (the driver never uses one; whether the chip has one is open: `rp-gpubench` run B) | mask `0x02265770`, table `0x0225D310`-`0x0225D39C` |
| **Non-power-of-two sizes are refused** (`GL_INVALID_VALUE`), as are sides over 2048 | confirmed (code) | `glTexImage2D` `0x02264070`-`0x022640a8`; 2048 check `0x02263a8c` |
| No pbuffer-as-texture (EGL 1.0 exports only); `glCopyTexImage2D` copies the framebuffer **on the CPU**, row by row, and twiddles it at the next use | confirmed (exports, CPU loop); likely (the set-up calls' meaning) | exports `0x02268f80`-`0x0226a914`; `0x02264ef4`-`0x02264fb4` |
| An invalid texture falls back to TEX0 `0xA0015600`, TEX1 `0xAE000000 \| addr >> 7` | confirmed (code) | `0x022674c8`-`0x022674f0` |

**`rp-gpubench` run A on the device (2026-10-19, twice, the same to a few
microseconds; `local/rp-gpubench-runA1.txt`, `-runA2.txt`).** Windows
Mobile's clock (`MBXCLK` `0x5`: the cores at 49.8 MHz; times follow the
clock, run 26), no board bits, nothing hung, the pool all given back.
Times are exact (the status bit watched), full screen = 2,400 tiles.
**The panel ran at 60 Hz** (`DISPPLL` `0xFC5`, set by `Display::adopt` as
in every program): a 16.6 ms frame, not Windows Mobile's 17.7 ms; the
image's own frame constant said 17.7 ms (corrected after the runs), so
its "fits in a frame" and W2's "late" lines used a 6% looser bound. W2's
1,534 flips in 25-26 s are 60 a second, one per frame.

- **G4a, rectangular textures: confirmed.** 64 x 32, 32 x 64 and
  512 x 1024 RGB565, TEX0's size codes apart, in the GLES driver's order
  (`tex::twiddle_rect`): 64 of 64 checked pixels showed their own texel,
  each shape. **A 480 x 640 layer is one 512 x 1024 texture (1 MiB at 16
  bits).**
- **G1, one full-screen quad over the background** (ms):

  | Quad | 32-bit target | 16-bit target |
  |---|---|---|
  | background only | 12.7 | 12.5 |
  | flat opaque | 13.7 | 12.5 |
  | flat translucent (alpha 0x80) | 24.9 | 24.9 |
  | RGB565 point 1:1 | 27.2 | 25.2 |
  | RGB565 bilinear 1:1 | 28.9 | 26.1 |
  | ARGB4444 translucent point / bilinear | 34.2 / 35.3 | 33.0 / 33.7 |
  | ARGB8888 point / bilinear | 29.6 / 33.5 | 26.2 / 30.8 |
  | PVRTC 4 bpp point / bilinear | 23.3 / 22.6 | 21.7 / 21.0 |
  | RGB565 bilinear, magnified 8x (64-texel texture) | 16.5 | 15.5 |
  | RGB565 bilinear, shrunk 2:1 / mipmapped 2:1 | 37.0 / 32.9 | 33.0 / 30.3 |

  **Texturing a whole tile costs about as much as the tile itself**
  (~5.3 us bare, ~11.3 us textured 1:1): a full-screen textured layer
  takes 1.5-2.1x a 16.6 ms frame (the panel at 60 Hz, as every program
  sets it: `Display::adopt`). Cost follows texel fetches: PVRTC (fewest
  bytes) and a magnified small texture are cheapest, shrinking without
  mips dearest, mips win back ~10%. A 16-bit target saves 0-3 ms.
- **G2, N full-screen layers** (32-bit, ms; 16-bit within 2 ms): opaque
  textured 27.2 / 27.8 / 27.9 / 28.4 / 29.1 / 31.5 / 35.8 / 40.0 for N =
  1-8 (**hidden opaque layers are almost free**: the core textures only
  what shows); translucent ARGB4444 34 / 49 / 74 / 94 / 114 / 133 / 153 /
  173 (**~20 ms each**); translucent flat 25, then +12.4 ms each. Not one
  full-screen textured layer fits a frame at this clock.
- **G3, partial frames:** under three translucent layers ~26 us a tile
  (a 480 x 16 row 1.57 ms, a 64 x 64 icon 0.85 ms, a 480 x 160 band
  15.5 ms, one tile 36 us); under one opaque layer ~11.4 us a tile (row
  0.69 ms, icon 0.37 ms). The 16-bit target the same.
- **G5, a layer's upload** (480 x 640, from SDRAM): the CPU in row order
  33 MB/s (16-bit 18.5 ms, 32-bit 36.9 ms, as `rp-mbx` found); into the
  512 x 1024 texture order on the way 18 MB/s (57 ms for the whole
  texture); reordered in SDRAM 37 ms, then DMA 34 ms (30 MB/s, the CPU at
  79% of its idle pace meanwhile); a 480 x 48 strip 3.0 ms into texture
  order, 1.4 ms in row order.
- **W1, a flip is not latched (owner's eyes): `GSCADR` takes effect at
  once.** Written at line 320 and back at 560 every frame, the owner saw
  a yellow band in the lower part of the screen with blue above and below
  it. Flips must be written in the blanking (VEVENT0, line 643), as
  Dell's are.
- **W2, double buffered, flipped at VEVENT0: smooth (owner).** A full
  render of the 480 x 560 test scene 11.3 ms; 1,534 flips, none late,
  every one written on line 643.
- **W3, one screen rendered in four bands just behind the scan line:
  smooth (owner).** Bands of 540 tiles 2.9 ms each; no band render was
  still running when the scan came back to it. One picture can be
  redrawn in step with the scan without tearing, saving the second
  1.2 MiB buffer.

**`rp-gpubench` run E on the device (2026-10-20, `local/rp-gpubench-runE1.txt`): faster uploads.** The bus is the 2700G's (`rp-cs3bus`), so the gains come around it. All checks right (the fast order matched the reference; the texture landed right; E4's white pixels 67,436 = the glyphs' lit pixels).

| A 480 x 640 layer into a 512 x 1024 RGB565 texture (1 MiB) | Time |
|---|---|
| G5: plain reorder in SDRAM, then DMA | 71 ms |
| G5: plain reorder written straight in by the CPU | 57 ms |
| **E1d: the reorder by 8 x 8 blocks from a table, written straight in by the CPU** | **34.8 ms (30 MB/s): 2.0x** |
| E1c: the fast reorder overlapped with DMA, piece by piece | 41.0 ms; the CPU waited (free) 11.6 ms of it |
| E1b: the fast reorder, then DMA | 46.0 ms |
| E1a: the fast reorder alone, SDRAM to SDRAM | 11.9 ms (the plain one 37.1 ms) |

The overlap gains less than hoped: the CPU's reorder and the DMA share SDRAM, so the reorder slows to ~29 ms beside it.

**E1e, staged in the internal SRAM (run E again, 2026-10-22 night, `local/rp-gpubench-runE2.txt`; the performance gate's T2):** each 32 KiB piece reordered into one of two SRAM buffers while the DMA moves the other from SRAM, so the CPU reads SDRAM and the DMA reads SRAM: **32.2 ms (32 MB/s, the bus's limit)**, the texture right, the CPU waiting (free) 7.4 ms of it; E1c (staged in SDRAM) 41.0 ms, E1d (the CPU straight in) 34.8 ms with the CPU never free. Same night: E1a 11.9 ms, E1b 46.0 ms, as before.

- **Fewer bytes (E2, E3), by DMA at 30 MB/s:** an 8-bit layer 17.0 ms, PVRTC 4 bpp 8.5 ms, 2 bpp 4.3 ms (2x, 4x, 8x).
- **An 8-bit (code 10 grey) layer draws faster than RGB565:** full screen 1:1 opaque 22.7 ms (565: 27.2), its texels as alpha 32.4 ms (4444 translucent: 34.2).
- **E4, text by 2D commands from a 4 bpp coverage atlas:** a full screen (1,406 glyphs) in 56 KiB of commands (pixels: 600 KiB, 11x more), but **20.4 ms** to draw (pixels by the CPU: 18.5 ms): the engine takes ~14 us a glyph command, and the CPU waits on the FIFO while queueing (19.7 ms). Fewer bytes, not less time, at one command a glyph.

**`rp-gpubench` run F on the device (2026-10-20, `local/rp-gpubench-runF1.txt`): text as 4 bpp coverage strips.** A screen of text (37 x 38 glyphs), white pixels exact by every method (67,436 = the glyphs' lit pixels):

| | CPU | 2D engine | In all |
|---|---|---|---|
| F0: RGB565 pixels drawn by the CPU, copied (600 KiB) | 22.4 ms (3.9 drawn, 18.5 copied) | - | 22.4 ms |
| F1-F3: 4 bpp strips drawn by the CPU, copied (142 KiB), coloured and blended by the 2D engine (Dell's text blend) | 6.2 ms (1.9 drawn, 4.3 copied) | 17.5 ms | 23.6 ms |

- **The 2D engine's coverage blend onto RGB565 runs at ~16 Mpx/s** (284,160 px in 17.5 ms), 3-4x slower than its plain copies and fills: one command a line (37) or one for all took the same, so the per-pixel blend sets it.
- So text as strips is **no faster in all**, but takes the CPU **3.6x less** (it runs while the engine blends) and the bus **4.3x fewer bytes**.
- **Run F, second run (2026-10-20, `local/rp-gpubench-runF2.txt`; F0-F3 repeated to the microsecond):**
  - **F4, a pre-coloured RGB565 glyph atlas in 2700G memory (made once, 1.7 ms, 36 KiB), a 2D fill then a plain 2D copy a glyph: 12.5 ms, 1.8x faster than pixels**, the picture word for word F0's; 56 KiB of commands over the bus; the CPU queueing the whole time (12.1 ms). ~8.9 us a glyph, of which the 192-pixel copy is ~3 us (the engine's 64 Mpx/s): the rest is per-command cost.
  - **F5, a 2D fill (3.3 ms) then the CPU writing only the words holding ink (57,318 words): 25.0 ms**, slower than pixels: scattered single-word writes cost ~380 ns each over the bus (a burst ~120 ns a word).
- **Run F, third run (2026-10-20, `local/rp-gpubench-runF3.txt`), F6: lean commands.** **The 2D engine keeps its surfaces between operations** (the destination and source words sent once, then only origin, header and rectangle a glyph: the picture word for word F0's), and **a plain copy runs without its colour word** (`OP_NO_COLOUR`): 4 words a glyph instead of 10. But the time stays **12.4-12.5 ms** a screen (56, 28 or 22.5 KiB of commands alike): **the engine spends ~8.8 us an operation** of which the 12 x 16 copy is ~3 us, so ~6 us is fixed per operation, on the engine's clock. **Repeated (`local/rp-gpubench-runF4.txt`):** every method within 0.5% of the run before, except F5's scattered single writes, 19.4 ms against 25.0: they vary run to run, a poor building block (the cores at half the system clock today: run G's divide-1 step would halve it if 2D runs right there, as version 29's 2D did).

**`rp-gpubench` run B on the device (2026-10-19,
`local/rp-gpubench-runB1.txt`): no row-order texture mode found.** A
512 x 512 RGB565 texture written in row order, drawn 1:1 with one TEX0
change at a time (20 tries, nothing hung):
- **bits 19-18** (1, 2, 3) and **bits 8-1** (each alone): no change, the
  texture still read twiddled (16 of 16 checked pixels as twiddled, 0 as
  rows);
- **bit 0:** something changed (pixel `0xff004408` where twiddled reading
  gives `0xff007963`), neither rows nor twiddled: its meaning is open;
- **odd format codes** (1, 3, ... 15): white, light grey or black, not an
  RGB565 reading (version 18 saw greys for 1 and 3): other texel formats,
  not a row order.
So every layer reaches the 3D core reordered (twiddled), by the CPU or in
SDRAM then DMA (run A's G5). Not proof the chip has no such mode: a mode
needing two bits at once, or a stride written elsewhere, wasn't tried.

**Run GC3T on the X51v (2026-10-24, the give-up audit's item 4, `local/rp-gpubench-runGC3T-card.txt`
from the CF card's `rplog/gpubench-0091.txt`; 1 min 46 s, unattended, the owner nearby):** each bit
of the render block's 47 other words (`0x600`-`0x6FC` less P2d's six, the target's and depth
buffer's place and size, and the start) set alone from 0, run P's texture drawn 1:1 into a 32 x 32
target: **all 1,504 renders drew the same picture in plain rows at the stride** (every target word
changed, nothing outside it, the rows decoding 1,024 of 1,024), none hung. So no single bit in the
render block twiddles the output: **render-to-texture isn't a switch in the render block.** Still
untried (not proof the chip has none): a mode needing two bits at once; run B's TEX0 bit 0 (it
changed the texture's reading, to neither rows nor twiddled); the 2D engine reordering a picture
into texture order (a copy, but on the engine, not the CPU).

**Queued device test: the video plane's repoint** (for ADR-0022 point 3's
translucent-layer-over-a-pan rule, scan-out blending first; **written
2026-10-19 as `rp-gpubench` run C (`RP_GPUBENCH_PLANES`) and run D
(`RP_GPUBENCH_VFMT`), not yet run**, runs only on the owner's go). Found
while writing it: the video plane's named formats are all YUV, so run C
has three watch steps: W4 the band test on `VSCADR`; W5 a list stored as
UYVY panned by `VSCADR` under per-pixel-alpha glass, with the same row in
RGB at the bottom to judge text against (the RGB to UYVY conversion
timed); W6 the planes swapped, the list in RGB panned by `GSCADR` under
the video plane as a tint at global alpha 0x90. Run D shows an RGB565
test card on the video plane at each unnamed format code 0-8 (colour
conversion off, the chroma bases pointed at zeroed memory first: mbxfb's
hang), one at a time until a button, the owner saying which show the
card right. W1 showed a `GSCADR` write takes effect
at once, mid-frame. The same test for the video plane: `VSCADR` written at
line 320 and back at 560 every frame (a band shows if at once), then a
pan by repointing `VSCADR` at VEVENT0 under a per-pixel-alpha graphics
plane (the glass), the owner judging torn / jumpy / smooth.

**`rp-gpubench` run C on the device (2026-10-19, twice;
`local/rp-gpubench-runC1.txt`, `-runC2.txt`; the owner's eyes).**
- **W4, a `VSCADR` repoint takes effect at once** (the owner saw the
  yellow band, both runs), as `GSCADR`'s does (W1).
- **W5, a UYVY list panned by `VSCADR` at VEVENT0 under per-pixel-alpha
  glass: smooth.** Its text against the same row in RGB: "the same
  mostly, maybe a little soft", which the owner put down to the motion
  ("it looked like it was supposed to look"). Judged while moving only;
  YUV text standing still was not compared. Making the 480 x 1280 list
  (text drawn pixel by pixel, then converted to UYVY) took 864 ms on the
  CPU: an upper bound on the conversion, not its cost.
- **W6, the planes swapped (an RGB list panned by `GSCADR` under the
  video plane as a tint at global alpha 0x90): smooth.**
- The list's jump from its last row back to row 1 every 640 lines is the
  test's (a 1,280-line buffer wrapping), not the hardware's.

**`rp-gpubench` run D on the device (2026-10-19, three runs, the third
photographed: `local/rp-gpubench-runD3-photos/`; `-runD1/2/3.txt`):
the video plane takes the graphics plane's RGB formats.** Repeated twice
more (2026-10-19, runs 4 and 5), run 5 photographed square-on
(`local/rp-gpubench-runD5-photos/`): every code the same. An RGB565 test
card (stride 960) shown with `VSCTRL` format codes 0-8, colour
conversion off; nothing hung, every code read back as written. What each
showed matches mbxfb's **`GSCTRL`** format numbers exactly:

| Code | On the screen (owner's photos) | Read as |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | half the card across the screen, greys | indexed, 8 bits (mbxfb's `INDEXED`) |
| 1, 3 | half the card stretched, greys (1) or pastel colours (3) | 8 bits a pixel (unnamed) |
| 2 | bars in greys at the card's width | 16 bits a pixel, unknown meaning |
| 4 | the card's layout, red and magenta off, the ramp striped | ARGB4444 |
| 5 | bars right, the ramp grey then blue from its middle | ARGB1555 (the RGB565 bits read one short) |
| 6 | one and a half cards across | RGB888, 24 bits |
| **7** | **the card exactly: eight bars, the ramp black to white (owner's eyes, run 5), the words** | **RGB565** |
| 8 | two cards side by side | ARGB8888, 32 bits |

So **the video plane is not YUV-only**: codes 4-8 are the graphics
plane's ARGB4444, ARGB1555, RGB888, RGB565 and ARGB8888 (7 proven by the
whole card, the rest by how the card was misread). Content panned on the
video plane can be RGB: no chroma loss, no conversion. Not measured yet:
the video plane's per-pixel alpha with an ARGB format (its blend modes
are the graphics plane's), and the strides those formats want (run D
kept 960). In all three runs code 0's step never saw a button press
(15 s each time) while 1-8 did: not the test's fault (runs 4 and 5 moved on at code 0 when pressed).

## The MBX cores' clock (static analysis, 2026-10-15)

From Linux mbxfb's register definitions (`local/re/mbxfb_reg_bits.h`,
`mbxfb.c`) and Dell's driver (`local/re/ddi_2700g/full.asm`). (A) until
`rp-mbx3d` version 26 runs.

- **`MBXCLK` bits 3-2 divide the MBX cores' clock** (0 by 1, 1 by 2, 2 by
  3, 3 by 4; bits 1-0 the enable). **Windows Mobile's `0x5` is divide by
  2.** The cores' clock is SYSCLK / divider; `SYSCLKSRC` is 2 under WM
  (Linux's `SYSCLKSRC_PLL_2`: the core PLL, likely); the PLLs give
  `13 MHz x M / (N x 2^P)` (`COREPLL` `0x038`, `DISPPLL` `0x03C`: M bits
  12-7, N 6-4, P 3-1), set by WM's boot code (Dell's driver never writes
  them) and never read yet.
- **Dell's driver picks the divider** (`0x021204f8`-`0x0212056c`): the
  system clock from its `CoreClock` registry setting (a PLL search around
  `0x0211f980`; literals 47, 66, 99/100, 121 and 133 MHz nearby), then for
  both `MBXCLK` and `M24CLK` the smallest divider from 2 to 4 keeping the
  clock **at or under 75 MHz** (`0x047868C0`, `0x02120724`); 1 if the
  system clock is already under it. So divide-by-2 is Dell's cap at work:
  the system clock lies between 75 and 150 MHz, and the cores run at half
  of it. Whether 75 MHz is the chip's rating or Dell's margin is not in
  the code. Linux mbxfb sets the core PLL to ~71.5 MHz and `MBXCLK` to
  `0x1` (divide by 1, 2D only).

**`rp-mbx3d` version 26 on the device (2026-10-15,
`local/rp-mbx3d-run26.txt`).**

- **S26a, the cores are clock-bound.** At `MBXCLK` divide by 2, 3 and 4:
  2400 tiles 12,484 / 18,724 / 24,968 us, 600 tiles 3,132 / 4,692 / 6,256
  us, a full-screen 2D fill 6,180 / 9,268 / 12,360 us: **exactly in
  proportion to the divider**, 3D and 2D alike. Every MHz of the cores'
  clock is speed.
- **S26c, the clocks:** `COREPLL` `0xBB1` = 13 MHz x 23 / 3 = **99.67 MHz**
  (mbxfb's own "100 MHz" setting), `DISPPLL` `0xEC5` = 23.56 MHz (the
  pixel clock), `PLLSTAT` 7 (both locked), `SYSCLKSRC` 2, `MEMCLK` 1,
  `M24CLK` 2. So **Windows Mobile runs the MBX cores at 49.8 MHz**, a
  third under the 75 MHz Dell's driver allows them; **Dell's default
  "CoreClock" is 133 MHz** (`0x021220f4`), which at divide by 2 gives 66.5
  MHz. Dell's driver sets the PXA's bus timing (`MSC1`) for the 2700G by
  the same clock: one setting for 99 MHz and up, which 133 MHz shares.
  Next: the core PLL stepped up, `rp-mbx3d` version 27.
- **S26b (T4), a 16-bit target: works.** `0x650` 1 wrote RGB565 (red
  `0xF800`, green `0x07E0`, blue `0x001F`, white `0xFFFF`), 3 wrote
  ARGB1555 with the alpha bit set (`0xFC00`, `0x83E0`, `0x801F`); tiles
  stay 8 x 16; **`0x660` is the stride in bytes / 4** in every format (240
  for 960 bytes; 480 skipped every other line). The 16-tile block took 120
  us, no faster than 32 bits as measured (a cost per tile, not per
  byte). Built: `PixelFormat` on the renderer's `Picture` (both models
  write 16-bit targets as measured). The interface's screen pictures stay
  32-bit (ADR-0023's choice, for exact 2D blending and dithering); making
  them RGB565 is an ADR-0023 change for the owner (half the memory and
  bus traffic of every picture; the 2D engine's blends onto 565 need
  their own device check).
## The MVED1 video decoder (static analysis, 2026-10-14)

Sources: `pvrvadd.dll` (16 KiB, the hardware layer: 30 `M24VA_*` decoder
and 9 `VDISP_*` overlay functions) and `wmv9decoder2700G.dll` (173 KiB, a
DirectShow Media Object that uses it), both from the A08 ROM (A), plus
`ddi_2700g.dll` for the parts behind its escapes. The decoder DLL is
byte-identical in all four ROM copies; `pvrvadd.dll` differs between ROMs
(not compared further). **`wmv9decoder2700G.dll` is UPX 3.02-packed**
(LZMA, an ARM call filter, relocations stored byte-swapped): it was
unpacked locally with a small script before disassembly (`local/re/mved/`,
never committed). Disassembled with `llvm-objdump`, labelled by a copy of
`local/re/gles-scripts/annotate.py` that also reads `ldr rX, [pc]`
literals. Findings only; no code copied. "M24" is the name the drivers and
Linux's `M24CLK` give this unit.

**Shape.** `pvrvadd.dll` reaches the hardware the way the GLES library
does: escapes to Dell's display driver set things up (open the device,
2700G memory, the clock), then the library writes the decoder's registers
and command windows itself, through mappings the display driver shares.
The decoder DLL is the codec: it parses the bitstream on the CPU and feeds
the chip a stream of macroblock commands. No interrupt is used: completion
is a status register the library polls.

### Where it is

| Finding | Confidence | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **Registers at `0x0FFE3000`** (4 KiB): the services layer's device type 2 is the 2700G base + `0x3FE3000`, beside the display's `0x3FE2000` and the MBX's `0x3FE4000` | confirmed (A) | `ddi_2700g.dll` `0x02120a94`; `pvrvadd.dll` takes its register base from the shared device structure's `+0x2c` (`0x021f665c`), as GLES does |
| **Two 4 KiB command windows at `0x0FFF0000` and `0x0FFF1000`** (one 8 KiB region at offset 0 of the 2700G's second base, `0x0FFF0000`; the MBX's windows are at `+0x4000`-`+0x6000` of the same base): the first takes **motion-compensation commands**, the second **coefficient or residual data**. This is the "slave port" | confirmed (A) | `ddi_2700g.dll` `0x02120ab0`-`0x02120ac4` (size `0x2000`); `pvrvadd.dll` `0x021f66a8` (window, window + `0x1000`) |
| Both windows sit inside the 128 KiB register window RetroPane already maps (offsets `0x10000` and `0x11000`) | confirmed (arithmetic) | above |
| The windows are written like the 2D engine's: sequential words at window + index, the index wrapping at **2 KiB** (512 words), one index per kind of data; never read | confirmed (A) | `pvrvadd.dll` copy loop `0x021f4564` (index mask `0x1FF`) |

### Clock and power

| Finding | Confidence | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **`M24CLK` (system `0x050`) is the decoder's clock**: bit 0 enable, bits 2-1 divider − 1 (Linux mbxfb's names) | confirmed (B, A) | mbxfb `M24CLK_EN`, `M24CLK_DIV`; Dell's clock vote, unit 4 |
| Dell's driver switches it **on** with `((divider − 1) << 1) \| 1` and off with the enable cleared; the divider comes from Dell's settings. **Windows Mobile idles at `0x2`: divider 2, clock off**, so its on value is **`0x3`** | confirmed (A); on value likely | clock vote `0x0211f5d8` unit 4 (`0x0211f670`-`0x0211f6a0`); `M24CLK 0x2` measured by `rp-wmgpu` (above) |
| The first vote for any unit also runs Dell's common power-up (`0x0211f58c`: a use count, and a power-state restore if one is pending); under RetroPane's loader the 2700G is already powered | likely | (A) |
| The clock is voted on by **escape 100013** (`0x186AD`, 8 bytes in: 1 and the caller's process id), sent by `M24VA_Initialise`. Dell allows **one owner process**, watches it with a thread, and votes the clock off (escape 100013 with 0) when it deinitialises or exits | confirmed (A) | `pvrvadd.dll` `0x021f4f78`; `ddi_2700g.dll` `0x02101b14`, `0x02101a78` |
| Compare the MBX: `MBXCLK` (`0x054`) bits 1-0 select 2D / 2D + 3D, the divider in bits 3-2 (measured, ADR-0024). `M24CLK` has a one-bit enable and the divider one bit lower | confirmed (B) | mbxfb header |

### Registers (offsets from `0x0FFE3000`)

Every offset `pvrvadd.dll` touches. **It reads only `0x50`, `0x80` and
`0xA0`** (the bus-hang rule: read nothing else). Addresses are byte
offsets in 2700G local memory (as for the 2D engine), always written
**divided by 8**. Sizes are in pixels, strides in bytes.

| Offset | Access | Written with / read as | Meaning | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `0x00` | W | 1, then 0 | soft reset pulse | likely |
| `0x10` | W | frame address / 8 | **output frame** (the one being decoded) | confirmed (A) |
| `0x14` | W | frame address / 8 | **reference frame 1** (forward; the only one the WMV9 decoder sets) | confirmed (A) |
| `0x18` | W | frame address / 8 | reference frame 2 (backward; never set by this decoder) | likely |
| `0x20` | W | `width \| height << 11` | frame size (11 bits each, as in `GSCTRL`) | confirmed (A) |
| `0x24` | W | stride / 8 | frame stride | confirmed (A) |
| `0x30` | W | source address / 8 | conversion source (a decoded 4:2:0 frame) | confirmed (A) |
| `0x34` | W | destination / 8 | conversion destination, mode 1 | confirmed (A) |
| `0x38` | W | `width \| height << 11` | conversion size | confirmed (A) |
| `0x3C` | W | mode 1: `(destination stride / 8) << 9 \| source stride / 8`; mode 3: source stride / 8 | conversion strides | **destination field corrected by the X51v** (2026-10-25, `rp-mved` v1: Dell's `/ 4` as read from (A) spaced the rows twice as far apart); source field (A) |
| `0x40` | W | destination / 8 | conversion destination, mode 3 | confirmed (A) |
| `0x44` | W | destination stride / 8 | conversion destination stride, mode 3 | confirmed (A) |
| `0x48` | W | 1 or 3 | **start a conversion**: 1 = 4:2:0 to YUY2, 3 = 4:2:0 to a 3-byte-a-pixel format (most likely RGB) | confirmed (A); RGB a guess |
| `0x50` | R | — | **FIFO levels**: bits 12-6 the command FIFO (31 entries), 20-13 the residual FIFO (63), 25-21 the coefficient FIFO (31); free space = (capacity − level) × 2 words | confirmed (A) |
| `0x80` | R | — | **status**: bit 0 conversion done, bit 1 frame done | confirmed (A) |
| `0x88` | W | the bits handled; 3 at reset | **acknowledge** status bits (1s clear) | confirmed (A) |
| `0x90` | W | 0 or 1 | 1 = the chip does the inverse transform (coefficient mode, below); 0 = the CPU sends residuals | confirmed (A) |
| `0xA0` | R | — | **capabilities**, low 16 bits: bits 0 and 1 are reported to the decoder | confirmed (A); meaning of each bit unknown |

**Sequences (A).** All run under a lock word shared with Dell's driver
(`+0xB0` of the structure Dell returns at connect, taken by
compare-and-swap with the process id). Every `M24VA_*` call fails with
"lost" (5) while that structure's `+0x80` is set (most likely Dell's
power-down flag); initialisation clears it after the reset.
`M24VA_GetSlavePortStats` and `M24VA_GetStats` only return zeros and the
`M24VA_SimScript*` functions are empty: a simulator hook compiled out.

1. **Reset:** `0x00` = 1, `0x00` = 0, `0x88` = 3; the library's window
   indexes go back to 0.
2. **Frame set-up** (once per stream): `0x20` = size. Frames are allocated
   in 2700G memory (escape 102003, 4 KiB aligned).
3. **Begin a frame:** wait until the display has finished with the output
   frame (a counter pair in Dell's surface record; 10 ms sleeps), then
   `0x90` = coefficient mode, `0x10` = output, `0x24` = stride, `0x14` =
   reference. Once per session the command word `0x1A800000` follows (a
   chroma position word for macroblock 0, 0; below).
4. **Feed commands** (below). The library stages up to 31 words and
   writes them when the FIFO has room: it reads `0x50`; with no room it
   polls 100 times, sleeps 1 ms, and gives up after 10 tries ("timeout", 7).
5. **End the frame:** command `0x78000000`; when the caller waits, poll
   `0x80` until bit 1 is set (100 reads, then a 1 ms sleep, 10 tries), then
   write 2 to `0x88`.
6. **Convert** (`M24VA_FrameConvert`, optional): finish the frame, set
   `0x30`-`0x44`, then `0x48` = mode; done is `0x80` bit 0, acknowledged
   with 1.

### Frames in memory

| Finding | Confidence | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Decoded frames are **planar 4:2:0 with the stride rounded up to 256 bytes**: the luma plane (stride × height), then **one chroma plane of height / 2 rows with the same stride, the two chroma components side by side** (the second starting at stride / 2): the IMC2-style layout Dell's overlay accepts | confirmed (A) | allocation `0x021f4178` (stride × height × 3 / 2); `VDISP_GetOverlayAddress` (luma, chroma, chroma + stride / 2); overlay formats above |
| Which chroma component comes first | unknown | the device test shows it |
| Conversion targets: YUY2 (2 bytes a pixel) and a 3-byte-a-pixel format, strides rounded up to 32 bytes | confirmed (A) | allocation types 1 and 2 |
| The decoded frame goes **straight to the video plane** (`VDISP_OverlayFlipSurface`, escape 100032); the WMV9 decoder never calls the conversion | confirmed (A) | its import calls |

### The command stream (`M24VA_WriteMCCmdData` / `M24VA_WriteMCCmdBlock`)

Words go to the first window; a "block" is just several words in a row.
The top four bits name the word. As found in the WMV9 decoder's
macroblock routines (`0x1000f734`-`0x1000fe9c`, `0x100123a0`-`0x10012630`):

| Word | Meaning | Confidence |
|---|---|---|
| `0x00000xxx` | **prediction set-up** for what follows: bits 1-0 the plane (0 luma, 1 chroma), bit 2 / bit 3 width 8 / 16, bit 4 / bit 5 height 8 / 16 (`0x28` a 16 × 16 luma macroblock, `0x15` an 8 × 8 chroma block), bit 6 and bits 9-8 flags from the vector mode, bit 10 a second vector follows | fields confirmed (A); flag meanings a guess |
| `0x080000xx` | **prediction block select**: bit 6 right half, bit 4 lower half (which 8 × 8 quarter, with four vectors a macroblock) | likely |
| `0x80000000 \| 1 << 30 \| y << 14 \| x` | **motion vector**, two signed 14-bit fields (x in bits 13-0, y in 27-14). The decoder adds 32 for the right or lower 8 × 8 block, so it is relative to the macroblock, most likely in quarter pixels; halved first when the stream uses half-pixel vectors | likely (units: device test) |
| `0x80000000 \| y << 14 \| x` (bit 30 clear) | the second vector of a two-vector prediction (only after a set-up with bit 10) | likely |
| `0x18000000 \| position` | **where the macroblock goes**: luma `0x1D000000 \| (row × 16) << 11 \| column × 16`, chroma `0x1A800000 \| (row × 8) << 11 \| column × 8` (row and column in macroblocks) | likely (bits 26-23 may be a size code rather than part of the position) |
| `0x10000xxx` | **residual block header**, before that block's data: bits 3-2 the block's x within the macroblock in 4-pixel steps, bits 5-4 its y, bits 9-8 the transform size (0 8 × 8, 1 4 × 8, 2 8 × 4, 3 4 × 4, WMV9's numbering), bit 1 intra (no prediction to add); one inter-frame path also sets bit 7 | fields confirmed (A); bit 7 a guess |
| `0x78000000` | end of frame (the frame-done status follows) | confirmed (A) |

A typical inter macroblock: set-up `0x28`, position, then for each
predicted block `set-up, 0x08000000 | block, vector` (three words: the
"MC command block"), then per coded block a header and its data in the
second window. An intra macroblock: set-up with no vector, then per block
a header with bit 1 and its coefficients.

### What the chip does and what the CPU does

| Work | Where | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Bitstream parsing, variable-length decoding (modes, vectors, coefficients) | CPU | the bit reader (`0x100880cc`) is called throughout |
| Dequantisation | CPU: the levels sent are already scaled by the picture's quantiser | (A) `0x10010300`-`0x10010320` |
| Motion-vector prediction, intra DC/AC prediction | CPU | (A) |
| **Inverse scan (zig-zag) and the 8 × 8 inverse transform** | **chip**, when `0x90` = 1. The CPU sends **run-level pairs** to the second window, one word each: bit 0 "last", bits 15-1 the run, bits 31-16 the level (`M24VA_WriteIZZData` / `WriteIZZBlock`). The scan order is chosen by a word `0x8000 \| mode << 13` (modes 0-3) on the same window (`M24VA_SetIZZMode`); intra blocks use mode 3 | confirmed (A) |
| The 8 × 4, 4 × 8 and 4 × 4 inverse transforms (WMV9 only), and the 8 × 8 one when coefficient mode is off | CPU; the result goes to the second window as **signed 16-bit residuals**, two per word (`M24VA_WriteResidualDifferenceData`) | confirmed (A) `0x10010478`-`0x10010510` |
| **Motion compensation** (16 × 16 and 8 × 8 blocks, one reference, sub-pixel vectors), adding the residual, writing the reconstructed frame | **chip** | the command words above |
| Loop filter, overlap smoothing, range reduction (WMV9's post-processing) | most likely CPU: the decoder takes CPU pointers to frames (`M24VA_FrameBeginAccess`, six call sites) | likely |
| Rotating the video when the screen is rotated | the **MBX 3D core**: `VDISP` allocates four rotated buffers and queues a job that writes MBX registers `0x65C`/`0x6EC` through escape 102022; this is the planar-YUV rotation job found in Dell's driver (2026-10-13) | likely |
| Scaling and colour conversion for display | the display controller's video plane | (B) |

**Completion:** polled. `pvrvadd.dll` imports nothing for interrupts; done
is `0x80` bit 1 (frame) or bit 0 (conversion), acknowledged in `0x88`.
Whether the unit can raise the 2700G's interrupt line (GPIO85, through
`MINTENABLE`) is unknown.

**Formats.** The decoder object registers for WMV1, WMV2, WMV3 (WMV9),
WMVA, WMVP and WVP2 (WMV9 screen and image), and MP42, MP43 and MP4S
(Microsoft's MPEG-4 variants), with outputs YV12, I420/IYUV, YUY2 and UYVY
(format codes in the image). The chip path was followed in the WMV9
routines; which of the others use it is not traced. The chip's tools (an
8 × 8 inverse transform from run-level pairs, sub-pixel motion
compensation, 4:2:0 frames) are also MPEG-1/2/4 Part 2's ("M24" may mean
MPEG-2/4: a guess), so **the unit may also serve MPEG-4 ASP (Xvid, DivX)
and MPEG-2** if its 8 × 8 transform is the MPEG one (IEEE 1180), or
switchable to it, rather than only WMV9's integer transform. That is the
first thing the device test measures (ADR-0025). H.264 is out of reach
either way (different transforms, intra prediction and deblocking).

**Escapes used by `pvrvadd.dll`** (for the record; RetroPane needs none):
100005 gamma (50-500), 100006 brightness (0-256), 100007 contrast
(0-256), 100009 the overlay's colour matrix (nine 16-bit values), 100013
decoder owner and clock, 100020 overlay attributes (52 bytes), 100026
screen rotation (90/180/270), 100032 overlay flip (12 bytes); the services
escapes 102001 (connect: the decoder is device type 3, the overlay types 2
and 6), 102002, 102003 (allocate 2700G memory: flags, size, 4 KiB
alignment), 102004, 102010-102014, 102017 (1 at connect, 0 at
disconnect), 102019, 102022 and 102023.

**`rp-mved` S0-S2 on the X51v (2026-10-25, the owner watching; v1
card log mved-0001 `local/rp-mved-S0S2.txt`, v2 mved-0002
`local/rp-mved-S0S2-v2.txt`).** The unit answers as Dell's code says:
`M24CLK` `0x2` under Windows Mobile, on at `0x3`; capabilities `0xA0` =
`0x04010003`; FIFO `0x50` = `0x15` (empty) and status `0x80` 0 before and
after the reset. **v1:** the YUY2 conversion finished in 128 us, row 0
exactly right, but only every other row of the window written and the
rest past its end into the guard: **the destination stride field
(`0x3C` bits 31-9) counts 8 bytes, not 4** (Dell's code as we read it
said `/ 4`). **v2, the field corrected: YUY2 exact** (8,192 of 8,192 bytes,
**U first**: `Y0 U Y1 V`), 128 us for 64 x 64; **mode 3 (3 bytes a pixel)
is BGR** (blue first), within 1 of BT.601 studio range in every byte, 200
us; guards intact both times, nothing written outside. Scaled, a 480 x 640
frame converts in about 10 ms (YUY2) or 15 ms (BGR) with the CPU free.
Status `0x80` reads `0x8000` after the first conversion's acknowledge
(bit 15 stays set; meaning open). Next: S3 and S4 (the decoder itself).

**Left open (for the device test, ADR-0025):** what `0xA0`'s two bits
mean; the chroma order; the vector units and the set-up word's flag bits;
the transform's exact arithmetic; which scan each `0x8000` mode selects;
reset timing; how fast a frame decodes.

**The 2D clock stopped for real (run FS, X51v, 2026-10-22, the owner
watching, `local/cardlog/gpubench-0044.txt`; quality audit C43).** FS1:
`MBXCLK` `0x5` to `0x4` (enable 0, the divider kept) with 20 full-screen
fills and a fence queued, 200 ms, nothing read from the MBX: the fence
hadn't landed; the clock back on, it landed 123.6 ms later and the work
was whole. FS2: **one read of `0x12c` with the 2D clock off never
returned**: the CPU's bus hung until the hardware watchdog reset the
device into Dell's restore screen (the card log ends at FS2's line, as
designed). So stopping the 2D clock is safe for queued work, and any MBX
register access while it is off is fatal: the driver must make that
impossible, not merely avoid it. FS3 (the renderer's path with the clock
off) wasn't reached, and as built it would read `0x12c` the same way.
**The guard (2026-10-22):** `clock::stop` holds the register bus while the
clocks are off, so such a read no longer compiles; FS3 now stops them with
the renderer idle and checks it recovers.

## The 2700G's local memory sets the clock limit (static analysis, 2026-10-15)

From Dell's driver (`local/re/ddi_2700g/full.asm`, its data read from the
DLL), after `rp-mbx3d` run 26 and the memory-timing session's note
(`docs/research/memory-timing.md`). (A), cross-checked against WM's
registers.

- **P3, every target format (`rp-gpubench` run P, 2026-10-21, X51v; log
  `local/rp-gpubench-runP.txt`, card log gpubench).** A 32 x 32 texture
  whose every texel names its place (red `x * 8`, green `y * 8`, blue
  `0xA0`, alpha `0xFF`) drawn 1:1 into a target at a 128-byte stride
  (`0x660` 32), 4 KiB of known pattern before it and 56 KiB after, with
  `0x650` set to each of 52 values; all 52 rendered, none hung, none wrote
  outside its rows (0 words changed before or after in every try):
  - **Bits 2-0 pick the layout**; codes 8-15 lay out like 0-7:
    | code | layout | first pixels (hex) |
    |---|---|---|
    | 0 | RGB555, top bit **0** (X1555) | `0014 0414` |
    | 1 | RGB565 | `0014 0814` |
    | 2 | ARGB4444 (alpha `F`) | `F00A F00A` |
    | 3 | ARGB1555, top bit 1 for opaque | `8014 8414` |
    | 4 | **packed 24-bit RGB888**, 3 bytes a pixel, blue first; rows of 96 bytes at the stride (768 words changed) | `A0 00 00  A0 00 08` |
    | 5 | **xRGB8888, top byte 0** | `000000A0 000800A0` |
    | 6 | ARGB8888 (Dell's) | `FF0000A0 FF0800A0` |
    | 7 | ARGB4444, the same as 2 for these opaque texels | `F00A F00A` |
  - **Bit 3 dithers.** Codes 10 and 15 (4444 with bit 3) differ from 2 and
    7 exactly where a channel falls half-way between two 4-bit steps
    (`x * 8` for odd `x`): rounded up at (odd x, even y) and (even x, odd
    y), a 2 x 2 checker; 565 and 555 can't show it here (every value of
    this texture is exact at 5 bits). The matrix's other thresholds are
    not measured (a ramp is needed).
  - **Bits 4-19** read back as written and changed nothing seen (bits
    4-11 with code 1, 4-19 with code 6); **bits 20-31 read back 0** (not
    stored).
  - **No code twiddles its output**: every one writes rows at the
    stride, so `0x650` gives no render-to-texture. Rendering into a
    texture still needs a reorder (or another register, unknown: P19's
    full read of Dell's code is where to look).
  - **P19's static read for render-to-texture (2026-10-21, `libGLES_CL`,
    the render table built at `0x022616c0`-`0x02261848`):** the GLES
    driver writes only codes 6, 3 and 1 to `0x650` (from the surface's
    format, `0x022616c8`-`0x022616d8`), and fixed words elsewhere:
    `0x61C` `0x00020007`, `0x614`/`0x618` `0x1E3CE508`, `0x644` 0, `0x648`
    1, `0x64C` 0, `0x6D0` 1, `0x6DC` 1; nothing in it depends on whether
    the surface is a texture. With the earlier reading (no pbuffer as a
    texture, `glCopyTexImage2D` copying on the CPU and twiddling later),
    **Dell's code has no hardware render-to-texture path**. What's left is
    the bits of those words nobody has set: `rp-gpubench` run P2's part
    P2d flips each bit of `0x61C`, `0x644`, `0x648`, `0x64C`, `0x6D0` and
    `0x6D8` alone and decodes the target as run P does.
  - **Run P2 on the X51v (2026-10-21, unattended; `runP2.txt`): 232
    renders, none hung.**
    - **Shapes (P2a):** codes 0-7 into a 16 x 16 target at a 96-byte
      stride and a 32 x 16 one at 256 bytes: all 16 in plain rows at the
      stride, none outside them. Still no twiddled target.
    - **Dither (P2b), a 64 x 64 grey ramp:** bit 3 dithers every 16-bit
      layout (codes 8-11 and 15: 555, 565, 4444, 1555), the 24- and
      32-bit ones not at all (12-14 as 4-6); bits off, every layout
      truncates. Each pixel is its level truncated or one step up, never
      anything else, and every place has a clean threshold: **a 4 x 4
      ordered dither, one matrix for all layouts**, in sixteenths of a
      step (4444), its top left at the target's first pixel:
      `11 3 9 1 / 7 15 5 13 / 10 2 12 4 / 6 14 8 16` (16: never up); the
      5- and 6-bit channels the same matrix at their coarser steps (5-bit
      `6 2 5 1 / 4 - 3 7 / 5 1 6 2 / 3 7 4 -`, 6-bit `3 1 / 2 -`).
    - **Alpha (P2c), strips at alpha 0, 0x55, 0xAA, 0xFF:** code 6 stores
      the source's alpha as drawn (0, 85, 170, 255) and, blended, the
      alpha blended like the colour (0, 28, 112, 255: alpha x alpha + the
      destination's x (1 - alpha)), so a layer rendered for later
      composition needs the alpha blended separately (not measured: a
      blend word for alpha alone); code 5 writes alpha 0; **codes 2 and 7
      are the same in every value** (4444 alpha stored: 0, 5, 10, 15
      drawn); code 0 writes the top bit 0 and code 3 writes it 1 whatever
      the alpha (fully transparent pixels too); codes 1 and 4 have none.
    - **Render-table bits (P2d):** 192 single-bit flips of `0x61C`,
      `0x644`, `0x648`, `0x64C`, `0x6D0`, `0x6D8`: none hung, 190 drew the
      target unchanged; **`0x644` bit 0 and bit 1 each changed where the
      pixels land**: half the target's words written, 32 of 1024 pixels
      at their own place. Dell's planar-YUV and rotation job writes
      `0x644` (above): likely an orientation (rotation or mirror) field.
      Its mapping wasn't logged (one line a try): a follow-up run logs it.
  - **Run T on the X51v (2026-10-21, unattended; `runT1.txt`).**
    - **The display's fetch, measured (T1)**, at Windows Mobile's clock
      (cores / 2), the same work with the graphics plane at 32 bits, 16
      bits (the same memory read as RGB565), stopped (`GSCADR` bit 31
      clear) and 32 bits again: **3D 37.9 / 35.6 / 34.3 / 37.9 ms; the
      CPU's 256 KiB read 38.1 / 32.5 / 31.9 / 38.1 ms**; `DINTRS` 0x5
      throughout (the display started again each time). So a 16-bit
      screen saves 3D ~6% (two thirds of the fetch's 9.5%) and reads ~15%
      (nearly all of their 16%).
    - **`0x644` is a 2:1 averaging downsample on write-out (T2).** With
      bit 0 the target gets half the rows, each the average of two
      rendered rows (row 0's green `0x04` = (0 + 8) / 2, row 1's `0x14` =
      (16 + 24) / 2); bit 1 the same across (half the columns, red `0x04`,
      `0x14`, ...); both, a 2 x 2 box average into a quarter of the
      pixels. The renderer's tiles stay the rendered size: the write-out
      is what shrinks. That is supersampled anti-aliasing's resolve
      (render at twice the size, written out averaged) and a free 2:1
      box downscale (thumbnails; each level of a blur pyramid). Dell's
      planar-YUV and rotation job is where `0x644` was seen (its scaling,
      likely). Bits 2 and up changed nothing (P2d).
    - **Its cost (run T3, `runT3.txt`): none; it saves time.** One
      full-screen render (2400 tiles, a Gouraud background and a
      400 x 560 textured quad, 32-bit), five times each: plain 15.95 ms,
      rows halved 14.51, columns halved 14.65, both 14.46 ms. Writing a
      half or a quarter of the bytes is ~1.4 ms quicker, so the write-out
      of a full 32-bit screen is ~1.5 ms of a 16 ms render. **So 2 x 2
      anti-aliasing costs the rendering of four times the tiles and
      nothing for the resolve** (the renderer holds 2400 tiles: a full
      anti-aliased screen is four renders of a quarter each, or the
      parts that need it), and a 2:1 downscale comes cheaper than a 1:1
      render.
  - **Run P2W on the X51v (2026-10-21, the owner watching, three runs;
    `runP2W.txt`, `runP2W2.txt`, `runP2W3.txt`):** colour bars and four
    ramps rendered into the background picture and shown on the graphics
    plane. Owner: **the 24-bit picture (code 4, shown as display format
    6 at 1920-byte rows) looked like the others** (so the planes scan out
    packed 24-bit, 3 bytes a pixel: run D's reading confirmed); **the
    dithered RGB565 ramps (code 9) showed no bands, very smooth**, the
    plain RGB565 ones banded. Render times (2400 tiles): 24-bit 21.45 ms,
    16- and 32-bit 15.78 ms (3-byte pixels write a third slower).
    **Also seen: a few white pixels in the black bar (top right) in the
    24- and 32-bit pictures, more in the 32-bit one, none in the two
    16-bit ones**: in memory or added at scan-out is open (P2W now reads
    the bar back after the render and after it was shown, and logs the
    plane registers).
  - **Run P2X on the X51v (2026-10-21, unattended; `runP2X.txt`): the
    specks are errors that change from render to render, in the 32- and
    24-bit targets only.** P2W's picture rendered five times a format,
    each over a marker fill, at Windows Mobile's clock with the 32-bit
    screen scanning out: bars wrong 37-46 pixels a render at 32 bits
    (3-5 of them white), 22-33 at 24 bits, **0 at RGB565**; against the
    first render ~630-700 pixels differed at 32 bits, ~420-440 at 24,
    **0 at RGB565 (all five identical)**. No pixel was ever left
    unwritten (the marker never showed). **A wrong pixel shows the colour
    of the next bar to its right** (magenta bar: red; blue bar: black;
    cyan bar: green), the next object in the scene's list: the 3D core
    reading another object's colour, or a write landing wrong, while the
    local memory is busiest (a 32-bit target written and the 32-bit
    screen fetched). Some places repeat between renders ((51,29) in four
    of five), most don't. Windows Mobile's screen is 16-bit: its memory
    timing may never meet this load. **Run P2Y (2026-10-21, unattended;
    `runP2Y.txt`) found the trigger: the display fetching a 32-bit
    screen.** The CPU's reads are exact (one render read back twice in a
    row, both passes: 0 of 307,200 words differed). Five 32-bit renders
    with the screen shown at 32 bits: 46-57 bar pixels wrong each,
    ~640 pixels differing from the first; **with the screen shown at 16
    bits (the same memory read as RGB565) or stopped: 0 wrong, all five
    identical**. So 3D renders into 32-bit pictures are exact unless the
    display scans out a 32-bit screen at the same time, at Windows
    Mobile's clock and memory timing. ADR-0023 (32-bit screens) is the
    owner's to revisit with this (a 16-bit screen: 0 errors, dithered
    ramps smooth to the owner's eye, 3D ~6% and reads ~15% faster; 32-bit
    layers and textures can stay, only the scanned-out picture changes).
  - Open: a separate alpha blend.
  - Uses (results only, the owner decides; neither is banked yet): a
    24-bit target (code 4) is 25% fewer bytes than ARGB8888 with the same
    8-bit channels but **no alpha** (opaque layers only), and is usable
    only if scan-out shows packed 24-bit: run D's code 6 ("one and a half
    cards across" from a 2-byte card) suggests the planes read 3 bytes a
    pixel, packed, but nothing has shown a 24-bit picture on a plane yet.
    A dithered RGB565 target from the 3D core itself (bit 3: run P2
    measured a 4 x 4 ordered dither on 565 and 555 too) would halve
    memory and bus against ADR-0023's 32-bit screens, and the display's
    own fetch (run W: the 32-bit scan-out costs 3D ~10%); how it looks is
    the owner's eye in run P2W, so ADR-0023 stays as it is until then and
    the owner's vote.
- **The system clock is also the local SDRAM's clock.** Dell's timing
  routine (`0x0211fb98`) turns the SDRAM table's nanoseconds into cycles at
  the system clock for **`LMTIM`** (`0x1018`; tRAS raised so tRAS + tRP >=
  tRC), sets **`LMREFRESH`** (`0x101C`) from the refresh period, and picks
  **`LMTYPE`**'s (`0x1014`) CAS latency by clock: CL1, CL2 at or above the
  table's first threshold, CL3 at or above its second.
- **Dell keeps ten SDRAM tables** (`0x0213F93C` on, 14 words each:
  thresholds, timings in ns, refresh 64 ms, **a maximum clock**), chosen
  by the `SDRAMMemType` registry value; **CoreClock is clamped to the
  table's maximum** (`0x02122334`).
- **The X51v's is the first** (`0x0213F93C`): CL2 from 50 MHz, CL3 from
  100 MHz, tRAS 48, tRP 25, tRCD 25, tRC 85 ns, **maximum 100 MHz**. It is
  the only table that gives what WM leaves: at 99.67 MHz tRC 9, tRP 3,
  tRCD 3, tRAS 9 - 3 + 1 = 7 (`LMTIM` `0x73392`), CL2 (`LMTYPE` `0xAC8`).
  So Dell's default 133 MHz CoreClock is clamped to 100, and the PLL search
  lands on 99.67 MHz: **the local memory is at its rated maximum now.**
- Consequences: raising `COREPLL` overclocks the local memory (and CL2 at
  133 MHz is out of any SDRAM's spec). Within Dell's limits (memory <= 100
  MHz, MBX cores <= 75 MHz) the faster MBX setting is a **75 MHz system
  clock with `MBXCLK` divide by 1**: the cores 50% faster, the memory 25%
  slower, and the PXA's bus timing in Dell's slower tier (25-99 MHz:
  `0x79D4`/`0x7DF1` against `0x2484`/`0x2481`), which slows the CPU's
  writes into 2700G memory. Dell's own divider logic picks exactly this
  for a 75 MHz CoreClock.
- **Device (`rp-mbx3d` run 27): the memory and both cores worked at
  133.25 MHz with WM's CL2 timing in a short pass.** Times scaled exactly
  with the clock (3D 12.50 to 9.34 ms, a 2D fill 6.18 to 4.62 ms). The PLL
  locked at once.
- **`LMTIM` fields** (mbxfb's header): tRAS bits 19-16, tRP 15-12, tRCD
  11-8, tRC 7-4, tDPL 3-0. WM's `0x73392` is 7, 3, 3, 9, 2. **`LMTYPE`**:
  CAS latency bits 12-10, bank size 9-8, row size 7-4, column size 3-0.
  WM's `0xAC8` is CL2, 4 banks, 12 row bits, 8 column bits. Linux mbxfb
  writes CL3 with the same timings at its "100 MHz".
- **For 133.25 MHz (7.5 ns), Dell's routine gives:** CL3 (`LMTYPE`
  `0xEC8`) and `LMTIM` `0x944C2` (tRC 12, tRP 4, tRCD 4, tRAS 9, tDPL 2).
  For refresh, WM's `0x615` (1557 cycles: 15.6 us at 99.67 MHz) gives
  11.7 us at 133.25 MHz. That is more often than needed, so it is safe.
  (Not used: the owner chose option A, nothing past the memory's rating.)
- **Option A's timing (74.75 MHz, 13.4 ns), from the same ratings:** CL2,
  `LMTIM` `0x62272` (tRC 7, tRP 2, tRCD 2, tRAS 6, tDPL 2), `LMREFRESH`
  `0x48F` (1167 cycles, 15.6 us). The formula gives WM's values exactly at
  99.67 MHz (host-tested in `clock.rs`). `rp-mbx3d` version 29 measures
  it.
- **`rp-gpubench` run G1 on the device (2026-10-20,
  `local/rp-gpubench-runG1.txt`): inconclusive; the method, not a clock,
  is in doubt.** Each step: the bus (a 1.2 MB flush, 256 KiB read back),
  4 MiB of 2700G memory in a pattern and its inverse, a full-screen 2D
  fill, a 3D scene against Windows Mobile's picture word for word.
  - 99.67 MHz, cores / 2 (Windows Mobile's): all right; flush 33 MB/s,
    reads 7 MB/s, 2D fill 6.5 ms, 3D 37.9 ms.
  - **49.83 MHz, cores / 1** (the cores at the same 49.8 MHz, the memory
    and bus at half): all right, but **slower**: flush 18 MB/s, 2D fill
    17.7 ms (2.7x), 3D 61.7 ms (1.6x). At an unchanged core clock, the
    2D engine and the 3D core lose speed with the memory clock.
  - 74.75 MHz, cores / 1: BAD: ~640,000 memory words wrong, the 2D fill
    never finished (200 ms), the 3D picture right; the bus at 6 MB/s and
    0.7 MB/s reads, slower than at 49.83 MHz: the system clock looked far
    below 74.75 MHz.
  - **108.33 MHz, cores / 2, CL3 (Dell's rule):** bus, memory and 2D right
    (the fill 6.0 ms, 9% faster); **every 3D pixel wrong** (`0xFFFFFFFF`).
    `rp-mbx3d` run 27 drew right at 133.25 MHz with CL2: CL3 itself is a
    suspect.
  - **The 60 s soak at 49.83 MHz / 1, the step that passed: BAD**, the
    same signature as the 74.75 step, and the 2700G stayed broken after
    its registers were put back (a black screen; one soft reset). So a
    step can leave the chip broken for the next one, and no failure here
    can be pinned on its clock.
  - **The PLL "locked" at the first poll every time**, even across a
    change of its frequency (99.67 to 74.75 MHz): whether `PLLSTAT` bit 2
    follows a relock is unproven. Since then `clock::set_speed` waits a
    fixed 2 ms after the bit.
  - The run's own accounting: a 2D fill that didn't finish was counted as
    `u32::MAX` wrong words and wrapped (fixed: a separate "hung" flag).
- **`rp-gpubench` run G2 on the device (2026-10-20): the log was lost.**
  The owner watched the screen cards: **steps 1-6 GOOD** (green, both
  repeats and their controls): 99.67 MHz with the cores at divide 1,
  49.83 / 1, 74.75 / 2, **74.75 / 1 (G1's failure)**, 65.00 / 1, and
  **108.33 MHz / 2 with CL2**. **Step 7, 108.33 MHz with Dell's CL3, never
  came back**: the screen went black and stayed so; no watchdog reset (so
  the CPU kept running: most likely a check crawling over a bus at a
  fraction of its speed, as G1's bad steps did), and a soft reset after
  several minutes. The PC had stopped waiting at 120 s (G2 runs longer
  than that even when healthy), and the soft reset cleared the run log
  in SDRAM. What it shows, from the owner's eyes only:
  - G1's failures at 74.75 / 1 and 49.83 / 1 didn't recur once the PLL
    got a 2 ms settle and every step was put back in full: G1's method
    is the likely cause, not those clocks (to confirm with G3's numbers).
  - **108.33 MHz runs right with CL2, and with CL3 doesn't** (G1: 3D all
    wrong; G2: no return). **Dell's driver never uses CL3 on the X51v**:
    its table's 100 MHz maximum clamps the clock below its CL3 threshold.
    So "CL3 above 100 MHz" is Dell's rule on paper only, and changing
    `LMTYPE`'s CAS latency on a running memory may need more than the
    register write: Linux mbxfb writes `LMPWR` active after `LMTYPE`
    ("enable memory controller") and then dummy reads, which Dell's
    routine doesn't.
- **`rp-gpubench` run G3 on the device (2026-10-20,
  `local/rp-gpubench-runG3.txt`): the run returned with its whole log;
  steps 1-4 right, then the control after step 4 stayed wrong and the run
  stopped itself (the owner saw 1-4 green).**
  - **`PLLSTAT` bit 2 follows the relock (V11 settled):** 99.67 to 74.75
    MHz read `0x7` once right after the write (the old "locked"), then
    `0xB` (not locked), and `0xF` (locked) 16 us later; 108.33 to 99.67
    the same; 74.75 to 99.67 and 99.67 to 108.33 showed no drop in 2 ms of
    reads (~1.5 us apart). So `set_speed`'s poll right after the write saw
    the stale bit: every "locked at once" before (run 27, G1, G2) was
    that. Fixed: the drop awaited first, then the lock.
  - **Writes to the memory controller can be lost.** After the four lock
    traces `LMTYPE` read `0xEC8` though `0xAC8` was written last; after
    step 4's put-back `LMREFRESH` read `0x48F` (74.75 MHz's) though
    `0x615` was written, and a second put-back left it so again. Written
    back to back on the 13 MHz reference clock, some writes don't hold;
    Linux mbxfb waits 1 ms after every register write of its set-up. Fixed:
    `set_speed` waits 1 ms after each memory-controller write, reads it
    back and writes it again until it holds (3 tries), and reports
    writes that never held (`Applied`).
  - **The texture was damaged, not (only) the clock:** the controls'
    wrong 3D pictures differed in the textured layer (`0xBF207860` for
    `0xBF205060`, 79,938 pixels): the texture, uploaded once, had changed
    in 2700G memory, so every later check failed whatever its clock (G1's
    carried-over failures likely the same). Fixed: each check compares
    the texture with its uploaded words, counts the damage, repairs it.
  - **The cores at divide 1 at 99.67 MHz (99.67 MHz cores, twice WM's,
    the memory at WM's clock): right, twice.** A full-screen 2D fill
    **5.35 ms (1.22x faster than 6.54)**, the 3D scene **25.7 ms (1.48x
    faster than 37.9)**; the bus unchanged (it follows the system clock).
    Dell's 75 MHz cap on the cores is Dell's choice: this X51v ran 33%
    past it here (one run: to soak and repeat).
  - 49.83 MHz / 1: as G1 (2D 17.7 ms, 3D 61.7 ms). 74.75 MHz / 2: bus
    18 MB/s, 2D 9.5 ms, 3D 50.2 ms; **74.75 MHz / 1: right** (G1's failure
    gone), 2D 7.7 ms, 3D 33.2 ms. The 2D fill and 3D aren't purely
    core-bound: at the same core clock they slow with the memory clock.
- **`rp-gpubench` run G4 on the device (2026-10-20,
  `local/rp-gpubench-runG4.txt`): all 28 steps ran and came back; every
  control GOOD; no memory-controller write needed rewriting (the 1 ms
  settle alone fixed G3's lost writes); no texture damage. The owner saw
  the screen go black right after the LOCK TRACE card and stay black for
  the whole run, though every render into it was right: the display
  stopped showing (open, below).** Each line twice, both passes alike
  (to within 0.2%):

  | System clock | Cores | CL | 2D fill | 3D scene | Bus writes / reads | Result |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | 99.67 MHz (WM) | / 2 = 49.8 | 2 | 6.54 ms | 37.9 ms | 33 / 6 MB/s | right |
  | **99.67 MHz** | **/ 1 = 99.7** | 2 | **3.46 ms (1.89x)** | **20.9 ms (1.81x)** | 33 / 8 | **right** |
  | 74.75 MHz | / 1 = 74.8 | 2 | 4.57 ms | 26.5 ms | 18 / 7 | right |
  | 65.00 MHz | / 1 = 65.0 | 2 | 5.21 ms | 30.5 ms | 18 / 7 | right |
  | 49.83 MHz | / 1 = 49.8 | 2 | 6.90 ms | 39.7 ms | 18 / 6 | right |
  | 74.75 MHz | / 2 = 37.4 | 2 | 8.24 ms | 44.5 ms | 18 / 7 | right |
  | 108.33 MHz | / 2 = 54.2 | 2 | 5.69 ms | 31.2 ms | 33 / 8 | right |
  | 117.00 MHz | / 2 = 58.5 | 2 | 5.27 ms | 28.9 ms | 33 / 8 | right |
  | 133.25 MHz | / 2 = 66.6 | 2 | 4.62 ms | 26.2 ms | 33 / 8 | right |
  | 108-133 MHz | / 2 | **3** | as CL2 | **every pixel wrong** | 33 / 8 | **3D wrong** (with or without the memory restart) |

  - **The best setting found keeps the memory at Windows Mobile's clock:
    the cores at divide 1 (99.67 MHz), 2D 1.89x and 3D 1.81x faster.**
    Dell's 75 MHz cap on the cores is Dell's: this X51v ran them at
    99.67 MHz right in 4 checks (to soak, repeat, and try warm and cold).
  - **CL3 breaks the 3D core and nothing else:** bus, memory patterns
    and 2D right, the 3D picture random colours, every time, at 108, 117
    and 133 MHz, with or without `LMPWR` and dummy reads after the
    change. Dell's "CL3 above 100 MHz" never runs on the X51v (its 100
    MHz clamp) and is wrong for this chip as RetroPane changes it; **CL2
    ran right to 133.25 MHz** (the memory 33% past Dell's rating: short
    checks only).
  - **The bus doesn't follow the system clock past 99.67 MHz:** writes
    stay 33 MB/s; reads rise only from 6 to 8 MB/s.
  - **G1's and G3's times were up to 2.6x slower at the same settings**
    (49.83 / 1: 3D 61.7 ms there, 39.7 here): the memory controller held
    lost writes then; G4's numbers repeat across both passes.
  - Open: `set_speed` reported every lock at 0 us though the traces see
    the bit low for ~16 us (the poll's timing; open). **The display
    stopped showing during the lock traces** (they still write the memory
    timing back to back, without the settle) and stayed dark while every
    later render landed right: a clock change can stop scan-out. Run G5
    checks the display after every change.
- **The dark display after run G4, found (runs R and S, 2026-10-20,
  `local/rp-gpubench-runR1.txt`, `local/rp-gpubench-runS1-healthy.txt`).**
  Dark (run R, as found): the sync generator ran at 60 Hz (the line
  counter moving ~39 lines a ms), every timing and plane register as in a
  working display, and no restart step brought a picture back (adopt, sync
  off and on, the display PLL, the whole set-up, the clocks put back).
  Healthy (run S, after a soft reset, every named register below the MBX
  block): the same registers, but **`DINTRS` `0x5` where the dark one read
  `0x15`: bit 4, the graphics stream's underrun (`STR1_UR`), set**, and
  **`DSIG` (the output signature) `0xFE6D32F` where the dark one read 0**.
  Also `PLLSTAT` `0x7` healthy against `0xF` after core-PLL changes (bit 3,
  open). So: a clock change holds the system clock on the 13 MHz reference
  for milliseconds; the display can't fetch its picture in time, flags an
  underrun, and sends nothing until the flag is cleared. Run U reproduces
  it (1, 3, 10, 30 ms on the reference) and clears the flag Dell's way
  (read, write back without the bit). The fix for any clock change: keep
  it short, do it in the blanking, clear the flag after.
  **Run U on the device (`local/rp-gpubench-runU1.txt`, `runU2.txt`): no
  underrun from 1-30 ms on the reference clock, from CL3 at 99.67 MHz for
  100 ms, or from G4's lock traces replayed; the screen never went dark
  (owner).** So the reference clock isn't the trigger. U6 found the lock
  traces' last writes lost again (written back to back: `COREPLL` read
  `0xBC1`, 74.75 MHz, and `LMREFRESH` `0x48F` after `0xBB1` and `0x615`
  were written last): the traces must use `set_speed`'s checked writes.
  The next suspect is load: G4 went dark at its first step, the cores at
  divide 1 at 99.67 MHz with the 2D fill and 3D render running ~1.9x
  faster, where G3 (slowed by lost writes) never went dark: the cores'
  memory traffic may starve the display's fetch (37 MB/s for a 480 x 640
  RGB565 plane at 60 Hz). `DMCTRL` (`0x10303010`) is the display's memory
  control, a candidate for its priority or FIFO threshold.
  **The owner, run U2: no black screen, but the picture glitched briefly
  in the middle of every card,** where each try parked the system clock
  on the reference (or changed the memory timing): the display's fetch
  falls short for a moment without the underrun flag staying set. Any
  clock change a user can see must be timed into the vertical blanking
  and kept shorter than it (~1.3 ms at 60 Hz: open, to measure).
  **Run V** (written) runs each kind of work alone at four clocks (WM's,
  the cores at divide 1 at 99.67 and 74.75 MHz, 133.25 MHz CL2) and
  reads the underrun flag after each, clearing it.
- **Bringing the dark display back (2026-10-20): runs G4 again, S, X,
  L and Z on the X51v.** G4 blacked the screen again (two in two). Run S
  on it against the healthy snapshot: of 146 registers only `DINTRS` bit 4
  (the graphics underrun), `DSIG` 0 and `PLLSTAT` bit 3 differ. Run X:
  clearing the flag (Dell's way) and then restarting the sync generator,
  the plane's stream and the whole display set-up: `DSIG` stays 0. Run L,
  a ladder: the pixel clock off and on, its source, the display PLL off
  and on (relocks), the local memory through self-refresh (**works: in
  and out**), the on-die frame buffer to sleep (**it slept and didn't
  wake: `ODFBSTAT` stayed 0x2**), the memory clock off 1 ms: none brings
  the display back. So the stuck part is below every clock and power
  switch. Run Z (`SYSRST`, then a rebuild from the healthy snapshot):
  **the panel went white (the reset took), then the 2700G froze the bus
  and the watchdog reset the device**, twice (the first try read
  registers at once after the reset; the second wrote only, Linux's way,
  and still froze). Linux writes `SYSCFG` `0xFFFFFF0C` ("setup a timeout,
  raise drive strength") right after `SYSRST`: run Z's third try does
  that and blinks the backlight per phase (no log survives the watchdog
  reset). Linux also sets `DMCTRL` `0xD0303010` where WM has
  `0x10303010`: the display's memory control, a suspect for the underrun.
  **Run Z's third try (`local/rp-gpubench-runZ3.txt`): no freeze.**
  `SYSCFG` `0xFFFFFF0C` right after `SYSRST` (Linux's bus timeout) is what
  a cold start needs; the clocks, the local memory (`LMPWRSTAT` 0: active)
  and 145 registers rebuilt and read back right (`DUCTRL` reads 1 where 0
  was written: an update flag). The owner saw the panel white with lines
  (the reset), then black, all 8 backlight blinks. The display sent
  nothing because the rebuild skipped `DHDET` (`0x500230`) and `DVDET`
  (`0x30283`), taking them for status: they are the display-enable window
  (80-560 x 3-643), 0 after the reset. Run Z's fourth try writes them.
  **Run Z's fourth try (`local/rp-gpubench-runZ4.txt`): the 2700G cold-
  started by RetroPane's own code, proven on the X51v (the owner saw the
  card):** `SYSRST`, `SYSCFG` `0xFFFFFF0C`, the clocks (sources, PLLs,
  enables), the local memory in Linux's `setup_memc` order, the system
  block, then the display's settings with `DHDET`/`DVDET`, the sync
  generator last; every write 1 ms apart and nothing read until the
  memory runs. `DSIG` live again. Next: the same on a display G4 left
  dark (the owner's check that it rescues one).
  The owner saw a black-and-white checkerboard on the panel once the
  display was back (blinks 7-8), before the card: **the reset doesn't keep
  the local memory's contents** (the picture, textures): a recovery redraws
  and re-uploads everything.
  **The rescue, proven (owner's check, 2026-10-20):** G4 left the display
  dark a third time (`local/rp-gpubench-runG4c.txt`; this time later in
  the run, after a Z rebuild had set `SYSCFG` to Linux's `0xFFFFFF0C`: so
  that setting alone doesn't prevent it), and **run Z brought it back**
  (`local/rp-gpubench-runZ5-dark.txt`: as found `DINTRS` `0x15`, `DSIG` 0;
  after the rebuild `DSIG` live, the owner saw the card). A dark display
  is recoverable without a soft reset: a full 2700G reset and rebuild.
  G4 after a Z rebuild also saw **72 hung 3D renders** (none before).
  **Corrected (2026-10-20, the logs read again):** all 72 are in the CL3
  steps, three a check (CL3 already broke every 3D render: before the
  rebuild they finished wrong, after it they hang); every CL2 step and
  every control drew right, 2D and 3D, so the cores' set-up (redone by
  each render's `bring_up` and the 2D engine's reset) came back whole.
  The 2D hang in the control after 74.75 MHz with the cores at divide 1
  is in `runG4b` too, with no rebuild before it: not the rebuild's. What
  the rebuild did leave behind: **the whole chip ran slower in G4's first
  pass** (99.67 MHz, cores / 1: 3D 25.7 ms against 20.9, 2D 5.3 against
  3.5; CPU reads 6 MB/s against 8; 49.83 MHz / 1: 3D 61.7 ms against
  39.7) **until its first step that restarted the local memory** (CL3
  with `LMPWR` written active and dummy reads); every step after ran at
  G4's speeds. Which part of that restart is needed is open: run Y
  (`RP_GPUBENCH_RESCUE`) measures before the rebuild, after it, after
  `LMPWR` alone, after `LMTYPE` CL3 and back, after self-refresh, and
  with `SYSCFG` back at Windows Mobile's 0.
  **Run Y on the X51v (2026-10-20, `local/rp-gpubench-runY1.txt`, card
  log `local/cardlog/gpubench-0003.txt`, the owner watching: it all
  worked):** every check GOOD, before the rebuild, after it and after each
  restart; the rebuild came back whole (display, 2D, 3D) at the same speed
  as before it. **But the chip was already slow before the rebuild** (the
  device fresh from run H2's watchdog reset and Windows Mobile's boot):
  cores / 2 at 99.67 MHz, 2D 6.5 ms, 3D 37.9 ms, 256 KiB read 38.1 ms,
  where G4b's controls ran 3D 34.3 ms and the read 31.8 ms. None of
  `LMPWR` alone, `LMTYPE` CL3 and back, self-refresh, or `SYSCFG` back to 0
  changed it (all within 1%). So the slow state isn't the rebuild's: it
  can come with a boot (G4b, fast, followed a soft reset; G4c and Y
  followed a rebuild and a watchdog reset: two cases, unproven), and in
  G4c it ended at the first step that changed the clocks (108.33 MHz, CL3,
  the memory restarted) and back. Open: what in that step ends it (a run
  repeating it alone, measured before and after).
  **Run G5 on the X51v (2026-10-20, `local/rp-gpubench-runG5.txt`, card
  log `local/cardlog/gpubench-0004.txt` byte for byte the run log, the
  owner watching):** every check GOOD in both passes, 318 renders, 0 hung,
  every control at 99.67 MHz GOOD, Windows Mobile's clock put back. **The
  cores at divide 1 are right above 99.67 MHz too** (CL2): 3D 20.9 ms at
  99.67, 19.2 at 108.33, 17.8 at 117.00, 16.6 at 133.25 (1.26x); 2D fill
  3.46, 3.18, 2.93, 2.62 ms. The CPU's flush stays 33 MB/s and the 256 KiB
  read 8 MB/s from 99.67 MHz up (the bus is the limit, as in G4). A 60 s
  soak at 99.67 MHz with the cores at divide 1: 37 rounds, 0 bad, the
  display's underrun flag never set. The run began in the slow state
  (99.67 MHz, cores / 2: 3D 37.9 ms, as run Y). **The screen went black

  **Run Z right after (`gpubench-0005`), the owner watching:** found
